The #5680 revision passes focused Linux CMake `test_staged_import_publish`, `test_vfp_assets`, and `test_prg_engine_data_io`; hosted cross-platform evidence for the revision is pending. | `HZ-data-corruption-01` -- this is the first command in the `EXPORT`/`IMPORT DATABASE` family that mutates database files on disk, treated accordingly: fail-closed-by-default overwrite consent, atomic same-volume staged commit, and full rollback on any failure. No index (CDX/IDX) or relation/container-metadata reconstruction; no `IMPORT DATABASE ... TYPE SQL` (tracked separately as issue #5473, blocked on this row); no schema migration/reconciliation against an existing differently-shaped DBC; owner-policy modernization only, not VFP9 parity. Protected cross-platform CI evidence (Windows/macOS `fs::rename`/`fs::create_directories` semantics) remains pending beyond the local Linux evidence recorded here. #5678 (found by an automated Codex code-review pass): `materialize_database_json_import_plan()`'s own destination-existence preflight checked exact-case `std::filesystem::exists()` paths only, so an existing case-folded alias (e.g. a pre-existing `CUSTOMERS.DBF` when the plan names table `customers`) was invisible to it on a case-sensitive filesystem, letting an import publish a second physical file representing the same VFP/Windows table identity -- not portable to Windows, and violating this row's own fail-closed overwrite-consent contract. Fixed by building a case-insensitive index of the destination directory's own existing entries once, up front, and checking every planned destination (the DBC itself, each table's own `.dbf`, and each table's own `.fpt` memo sidecar) against it in addition to the exact-case check. `build_database_sql_import_plan()` (the `TYPE SQL` import planner) shares this identical materializer at the runtime dispatch layer, so this one fix closes the gap for both `TYPE JSON` and `TYPE SQL` imports without a separate change. New regression test (`test_materialize_database_json_import_plan_rejects_case_folded_collisions`, covering all three collision points) verified to reliably fail against the pre-fix code (9 total failures) and reliably pass against the fix. A review round on the fix's own PR found two further gaps: (1) the destination directory's own scan silently discarded a `fs::directory_iterator` construction or increment error, treating a directory that permits write/traversal but not listing as empty and defeating the fail-closed guarantee precisely when the scan is unavailable -- fixed by returning a new `Vfp.AssetInspector.Error.DatabaseImportDestinationScanFailed` failure on any scan error instead; (2) two of the new test's own assertions checked `fs::exists()` on a path that is itself a case-folded alias of a pre-existing file, which is unconditionally true on a case-insensitive filesystem regardless of the fix's correctness -- fixed by asserting directory-entry-count and pre-existing-file-content/size invariance instead. This is one of five related defects an automated review pass found in this same function (#5678/#5679/#5680/#5681/#5682); this fix addresses #5678 only -- #5679/#5680's own TOCTOU races on the commit/rollback identity binding, and #5681/#5682's own ignored-cleanup-error gaps, remain open, tracked separately. #5745 (found by the same review pass against the merged #5678 fix itself): the destination-directory scan added for #5678 retained every directory entry in memory regardless of whether it matched a planned destination, so memory scaled with destination-directory cardinality (measured ~98 MiB RSS for 200,000 unrelated files) rather than with the import plan's own size. Fixed by deriving the plan's own small, bounded set of case-folded destination basenames (the DBC itself, each table's `.dbf`, each table's `.fpt`) before scanning, and discarding any directory entry that does not match one of them instead of retaining it. Verified with a standalone reproduction outside the test suite: 50,000 unrelated files produced a measured 24 MiB RSS delta before this fix and a 0 KiB delta after. `test_materialize_database_json_import_plan_ignores_unrelated_directory_entries` proves the functional-correctness side (a legitimate import still succeeds, and a real collision is still detected, among thousands of unrelated entries). #5828 (found by an automated Codex code-review pass reviewing the merged #5745 fix itself): that fix's own `for (; scan_it != scan_end; scan_it.increment(scan_error)) { if (scan_error) {...} ... }` loop checked `scan_error` only at the top of the *next* iteration's body -- but `directory_iterator::increment()` sets the iterator equal to its own end sentinel on failure (its documented contract), so a failing increment made the loop's own condition false and exited the loop before that iteration's body, and therefore its `scan_error` check, ever ran. A scan error partway through the destination directory was silently accepted as a complete listing, defeating the exact fail-closed guarantee the scan exists to provide. Fixed by replacing the `for` loop with a `while` loop that checks `scan_error` immediately after each `increment()` call, which cannot be skipped by the loop's own condition re-evaluation -- a structural fix verifiable by inspection against `directory_iterator::increment(error_code&)`'s own documented contract, rather than requiring a live reproduction of an inherently racy, hard-to-force-deterministically OS-level directory read failure. #5828 also flagged the scan's own lack of a bound (a huge or slow mounted directory could keep an import in preflight indefinitely); fixed by adding a `kMaxDestinationScanEntries` cap (1,000,000, generously above the ~200,000-entry adversarial case #5745 itself was reported against) with a new localized `Vfp.AssetInspector.Error.DatabaseImportDestinationScanTooLarge` diagnostic -- not verified with a dedicated large-file test given the prohibitive runtime of creating over a million files, but the bound-check itself is a single integer comparison, low-risk to get wrong. A further PR review round (chatgpt-codex-connector, P2) correctly noted this is an *entry-count* bound only, not a wall-clock time bound -- a single slow or unresponsive `directory_iterator::increment()` call (e.g. against a hung network mount) can still block indefinitely before the counter is ever consulted again, regardless of the directory's actual entry count; a genuine deadline-controlled or interruptible enumeration would need machinery `std::filesystem` does not provide on its own (there is no way to cancel an in-flight blocking directory read from another thread), and was not attempted -- the code comment and this row were corrected to claim only entry-count protection, not a time bound. #5828's third claim -- that publication via `create_hard_link()` after this scan can still be raced by a case-aliasing file created during staging -- is the same TOCTOU shape already tracked separately (#5679/#5680, needing fd-relative/handle-based identity binding rather than pathname re-resolution) and was not attempted here. #5679/#5680 (found by the same review pass): the commit loop published each staged file via plain path-based `fs::create_hard_link(staged_path, final_path)` and, on any later commit failure, rolled back every already-committed entry via unconditional `fs::remove(final_path)` -- neither operation verified that the name it acted on still referred to the exact object staged/published earlier, so (#5680) a staged file replaced between its own creation and the commit loop reaching it could have its substituted content published under the original, verified name, and (#5679) a final destination replaced by an unrelated concurrent actor after this transaction published it there could be deleted by rollback instead of preserved. Fixed by a new dedicated module (`include/copperfin/vfp/staged_import_publish.h`, `src/vfp/staged_import_publish.cpp`) that opens and identity-pins every staged file (table, memo sidecar, and catalog alike) immediately after it is written, from staging through publication and any later rollback: on Windows the open handle denies other processes write/delete/rename sharing for its whole lifetime (`FILE_SHARE_READ` only), making the race impossible rather than merely detected, and rollback deletes strictly through a handle-based `FILE_DISPOSITION_INFO` set only after re-confirming the destination's volume-serial/file-index identity still matches. On POSIX, no primitive lets a descriptor-bound publish survive its last directory entry being removed (a `/proc/self/fd`/`/dev/fd` + `linkat(..., AT_SYMLINK_FOLLOW)` resurrection attempt was tried and empirically confirmed not to work once an inode's link count reaches zero -- the kernel deliberately blocks it outside the `CAP_DAC_READ_SEARCH`/`O_TMPFILE` case), so publication instead re-verifies `staged_path`'s device/inode identity against what was captured at staging time immediately before linking, failing the entry closed on any mismatch rather than linking either stale or substituted content; rollback removal is the same re-check-then-act pattern against the destination. Both POSIX paths keep the original descriptor open for their entire lifetime specifically to prevent the kernel from recycling that exact inode number onto an unrelated file in the interim, which would otherwise let a later identity re-check be fooled; a narrow residual race remains between each re-check and its corresponding `link()`/`unlink()` syscall, which is the best available mitigation in standard POSIX without additional privilege, and is documented as such in the module's own comments rather than overclaimed as fully closed. A commit-loop rollback entry that cannot be safely reclaimed now returns a distinct `Vfp.AssetInspector.Error.DatabaseImportCommitFailedUnreclaimedEntry` diagnostic instead of being silently skipped. Verified via a new dedicated `test_staged_import_publish` suite exercising both core security properties directly and deterministically (no threading required, since the module's own API lets a test open a handle, then swap the underlying file out from under it before invoking the publish/rollback call being tested): a staged-file swap after opening is confirmed to make `publish_staged_import_file()` fail closed rather than publish either the original or substituted bytes, and a destination replaced after publish is confirmed to survive `remove_published_import_file_if_identity_matches()` untouched rather than being deleted -- both confirmed via fail-then-pass (the rollback-preservation property was verified to fail against a reverted blind-`unlink()` implementation and pass against the fix; the publish property's own first implementation attempt, the `/proc`/`/dev` fd-relative `linkat()` approach, was caught failing this same test before being replaced with the re-check-based fix). The full existing `test_vfp_assets` materializer suite (round-trips, memo handling, case-folded collisions, path traversal, table-name collisions) continues to pass unchanged, confirming no regression to the ordinary non-adversarial import path. #5681/#5682's own ignored-cleanup-error gaps in the same function remain open, tracked separately, as does #5828's cross-referenced note above about a case-aliasing file created during staging specifically (a different, not-yet-addressed collision shape from the identity-binding races this fix closes). #5681/#5682 (found by the same review pass; **partial fix landed, both issues left open**): both the successful-import staging cleanup and the rollback-side staged-file/staging-directory cleanup used `std::error_code ignored;` for every removal, so a genuine removal failure (e.g. a locked file, a permission issue, a still-open handle) was silently discarded and the command reported either unqualified success (#5681) or one generic commit-failed message implying full rollback (#5682) regardless of whether staged aliases to the imported data actually remained on disk. Fixed the "silently ignores/misreports" half of both issues: `release_and_remove_staged_files()` (a new function in the `staged_import_publish` module, directly unit-testable via denying the staging directory's own permissions to force a real removal failure -- verified fail-then-pass against a reverted always-`true` implementation) now returns whether every staged file and the staging directory itself were actually removed. `DatabaseJsonImportResult` gained `cleanup_incomplete`/`cleanup_warning` fields, set when a successful import's own staging cleanup did not fully complete (#5681), surfaced to the PRG runtime as a distinct `runtime.import_database_cleanup_incomplete` event rather than folded into the plain success event. The rollback path (#5682) now also checks staged-file/staging-directory cleanup success and returns a new, distinct `Vfp.AssetInspector.Error.DatabaseImportCommitFailedStagingCleanupIncomplete` diagnostic when it fails (subordinate in priority to the existing `...UnreclaimedEntry` diagnostic from the #5679/#5680 fix, since a still-committed final-path destination is the more severe of the two possible residual states). **Explicitly not attempted**: both issues' own central "persist bounded, identity-bound recovery state so a later invocation can safely retry cleanup after a process restart, and recognize its own leftovers on the next import into the same destination rather than treating them as an unrelated collision" requirement -- this is a materially larger, separate feature (a durable recovery-state file format, a dedicated recovery/reconciliation routine, and detection wiring at the start of a later import) that was judged too large to attempt in the same sitting as the achievable, real "stop silently discarding cleanup errors" fix landed here; both issues are left open rather than closed to track that remaining, explicitly out-of-scope work. #5680 remains open: Linux publication now binds to the retained descriptor and Windows retains exclusive sharing, while macOS retains a pathname recheck/link race against a same-authority actor. Network and removable-volume behavior also needs hosted or platform-specific evidence before closure. Missing destination parent directories are tracked and removed on failure when still empty. The digest proof covers writer-close/handle-open replacement, and Windows retains no-delete-share directory-chain handles through staging. | `defined` |

It shall parse only the exact, narrow SQL dialect `EXPORT DATABASE ... TYPE SQL` itself emits: the fixed three-line header comment (used as an up-front subset gate before any token is read), double-quoted identifiers with doubled-double-quote escaping, single-quoted string literals with doubled-single-quote escaping, and `CREATE TABLE`/`INSERT INTO` statements using exactly that exporter's column-type vocabulary (`VARCHAR`, `DECIMAL`, `INTEGER`, `DOUBLE PRECISION`, `BOOLEAN`, `DATE`, `TIMESTAMP`, `TEXT`). It is explicitly not a general-purpose SQL parser: any document missing the exact header, using unquoted identifiers, an unrecognized column type, an `INSERT` referencing an undefined table or column, a `VALUES` count mismatching its column list, or a literal shape wrong for its column's type (a string for a numeric column, for example) shall be rejected with a distinct `error_code` before any file is written, rather than guessed at. Because the SQL dialect's type vocabulary is coarser than the DBF storage types it was derived from, some field-type precision is intentionally lost on the round trip, matching the same narrowing `export_database_as_sql()` already performs on the way out: `N`, `F`, and `Y` fields all become `N` on reimport, and `M`, `G`, and `P` fields all become `M`. `import_database_command`'s existing `TYPE` dispatch is extended to route `TYPE SQL` to this parser and `TYPE JSON` to the existing one, sharing one syntax/diagnostic/event contract with only the plan-construction step and its own distinct `runtime.import_database_sql` event differing from the JSON path. | `include/copperfin/vfp/asset_inspector.h`; `src/vfp/asset_inspector.cpp`; `src/runtime/prg_engine_dispatch.inl`; `resources/locales/*/strings.json` | `tests/test_prg_engine_data_io_import_export.cpp`; `tests/test_vfp_assets.cpp`; broader protected native validation remains release evidence | Focused `test_prg_engine_data_io` (`test_import_database_type_sql_round_trips_via_export`) proves the full PRG-script path: a real `EXPORT DATABASE ... TYPE SQL` snapshot re-imported via `IMPORT DATABASE ... | `RQ-CF-MODERNIZATION-006` | Explicit repository-owner product policy in #140/#141, scoped as issue #5475 (phase 1 of #141's `EXPORT DATABASE ... TYPE ACCESS`); derived from `RQ-CF-MODERNIZATION-003` and grounded in `docs/66-access-container-format-notes.md`'s finding that the *logical* Access SQL/DDL dialect is citable public documentation, distinct from the physical MDB/ACCDB byte format, which is not | Copperfin shall provide the non-VFP-extension command `EXPORT DATABASE <quoted-dbc-path> TO <quoted-output-path> TYPE ACCESS`, sharing `EXPORT DATABASE ... TYPE JSON`/`TYPE SQL`'s DBC catalog/table-resolution loader so all three `TYPE` variants cannot silently disagree on which tables/rows constitute the database. It shall resolve relative paths from the current default directory, append `.sql` only when the destination has no extension (this is a SQL-script export, explicitly not a native `.accdb`/`.mdb` binary writer), emit `runtime.export_database_access_sql` only after a successful write, and preserve `export_database_command` as its shared package-debug IR opcode with `TYPE JSON`/`TYPE SQL`. It shall emit the Access/Jet SQL dialect rather than `TYPE SQL`'s portable/ANSI-ish one: square-bracket `[identifier]` quoting with an embedded `]` escaped by doubling (the Jet/ACE bracket-escape convention -- the exporter's input is DBC/DBF catalog metadata a crafted or corrupt source is not bound to keep free of `]`, unlike a real Access UI), single-quoted string literals with embedded-quote doubling (unchanged from `TYPE SQL`), `CREATE TABLE` per table mapping DBF field types to Access-native column types (`TEXT(length)` for a Character field of 255 characters or fewer and `MEMO` above that ceiling or for memo/general/picture pointer types, `DECIMAL(precision, scale)` for numeric/float with precision clamped to `[1, 28]` (Access's own DECIMAL precision ceiling) and scale clamped to at most that precision rather than trusting an untrustworthy header, `CURRENCY` for VFP currency -- an exact fixed-point match rather than `TYPE SQL`'s `DECIMAL(19, 4)` approximation, `LONG` for VFP integer, `DOUBLE` for VFP double, `YESNO` for logical, and `DATETIME` for both date and date-time fields, since Access has no separate date-only storage type), followed by `INSERT` statements per row using `#...#`-delimited ISO-form (`#YYYY-MM-DD[ HH:MM:SS]#`) date/time literals instead of quoted strings. A numeric field's decoded value is emitted unquoted only when it is a safe plain optionally-signed decimal literal; anything else (a numeric-overflow marker such as `*****`, or crafted/corrupted content) becomes `NULL` instead, since an unquoted numeric token has no quoting layer to escape injected SQL with the way a string literal does. Unlike `TYPE SQL`, no `-- ...` comment lines (header/provenance or skipped-table) are emitted at all: independently verified research found native Jet/ACE SQL has no supported in-band comment syntax at all (neither interactively through Access's SQL View nor via a DAO/ADO `Execute()` call), so embedding one would make the generated script fail exactly where this exporter's purpose is to succeed; a skipped/unreadable table contributes nothing to the output rather than a diagnostic comment. A destination resolving to the same file as the source (e.g. an explicit-extension destination equal to the source path, which skips the `.sql`/`.json` default) shall be rejected before any read or write, for this and both sibling `TYPE` variants, sharing one check at the common dispatch layer -- otherwise a successful read followed by output-truncation would irreversibly destroy the source database. Stored procedures for exported queries and per-vendor procedural SQL translation (PL/pgSQL, T-SQL, PL/SQL, MySQL's procedure syntax) are an explicit non-goal for this issue's first slice, deferred until a saved-query/view SQL source actually exists to translate from (see #5538, itself only partially complete -- SQL view text extraction remains open). It shall resolve relative paths from the current default directory, append `.sql` only when the destination has no extension, emit `runtime.export_database_sqlserver_sql` only after a successful write, and preserve `export_database_command` as its shared package-debug IR opcode. Unlike `TYPE SQLITE` (which reuses `write_sql_tables_and_data()` verbatim), this exporter has its own dedicated table/data writer entirely (the same reason `TYPE ACCESS` does) since T-SQL's dialect diverges enough from the portable/ANSI-ish baseline: square-bracket `[identifier]` quoting (`]` doubled to escape, matching `TYPE ACCESS`'s own bracket-escaping convention), `MONEY` for VFP currency (an exact match for its own fixed 4-decimal-digit scaled-integer semantics, the same reasoning `TYPE ACCESS` applies to `CURRENCY`), `BIT` for logical (T-SQL has no `BOOLEAN` type, and its `BIT` column literal must be `1`/`0` rather than the `TRUE`/`FALSE` keyword the other three ANSI-family dialects all accept -- directly confirmed against the real engine: `INSERT ... VALUES (TRUE)` fails with "Invalid column name 'TRUE'", since T-SQL has no boolean-literal syntax outside a predicate context), `DATE`/`DATETIME2` for VFP date/datetime (`DATETIME2` specifically, Microsoft's own documented modern replacement for the legacy `DATETIME` type), `VARCHAR(length)` for character fields, and `VARCHAR(MAX)` -- not the deprecated `TEXT` type -- for memo/general/picture and any other unrecognized storage type. A blank VFP date field shall emit `NULL`, not an empty string literal (#5554 PR review, chatgpt-codex-connector, P1: directly confirmed against the real engine that `CAST('' AS DATE)` silently returns `1900-01-01` rather than erroring, so the empty-string form would silently invent a date that was never there). A numeric field's `DECIMAL` precision shall be clamped to SQL Server's own 38-digit maximum and its scale clamped to that same precision (#5554 PR review, chatgpt-codex-connector and copilot-pull-request-reviewer, independently: a crafted/corrupt DBF header's raw `uint8_t` length/decimal_count is not bound to stay in range the way this codebase's own writer keeps it, and real SQL Server rejects `DECIMAL(39, 0)` outright while accepting `DECIMAL(38, 38)`, both directly confirmed against the real engine; `access_column_type()` already applies the identical clamp pattern for Access's own 28-digit ceiling). It shall add the same `CREATE INDEX` generation `RQ-CF-MODERNIZATION-007` already implements (same plain-column-reference vs. composite-expression-skip logic; T-SQL's own `CREATE INDEX` syntax is identical to PostgreSQL's for this case), with index names disambiguated (`disambiguate_index_name()`, reusing #5559's own helper) truncated to SQL Server's own 128-*character* identifier limit -- directly confirmed the real engine *rejects* (error 103) rather than silently truncates an over-length identifier, a materially different failure mode from PostgreSQL's own silent truncation -- but, unlike `RQ-CF-MODERNIZATION-007`'s/`RQ-CF-MODERNIZATION-008`'s schema-wide (whole-export) disambiguation, scoped to a fresh set *per table*: directly confirmed against the real engine that T-SQL index names are unique only within their own table (two different tables may carry an identically-named index, and a table may share a name with an unrelated table's own index, with no error either way), so the only collision that can still arise is truncation itself erasing a tag-derived suffix when a table name alone is already at or beyond the 128-character limit. Because SQL Server's limit is a *character* count, not a byte count (#5554 PR review, chatgpt-codex-connector, P2), truncation shall count Unicode code points rather than raw UTF-8 bytes (`disambiguate_index_name()`'s new `IdentifierLengthUnit` parameter, `utf8_safe_truncate()`); this same review pass found and fixed the identical latent defect in `RQ-CF-MODERNIZATION-007`'s own byte-based PostgreSQL truncation, which could split a multi-byte UTF-8 sequence in half -- `utf8_safe_truncate()`'s byte-mode now backs off to the last complete character boundary instead, a correction to `RQ-CF-MODERNIZATION-007`'s implementation applied in this same commit even though `RQ-CF-MODERNIZATION-007` itself is not otherwise revised here. A destination resolving to the same file as the source shall be rejected before any read or write, sharing the existing check at the common dispatch layer. TYPE JSON`/`TYPE SQL`/`TYPE ACCESS`/`TYPE POSTGRESQL`/`TYPE SQLITE`/`TYPE SQLSERVER`'s DBC catalog/table-resolution loader so all seven `TYPE` variants cannot silently disagree on which tables/rows constitute the database. It shall resolve relative paths from the current default directory, append `.sql` only when the destination has no extension, emit `runtime.export_database_oracle_sql` only after a successful write, and preserve `export_database_command` as its shared package-debug IR opcode. Like `TYPE SQLSERVER` (which reuses no part of `write_sql_tables_and_data()`), this exporter has its own dedicated table/data writer entirely, since Oracle's dialect diverges enough from the portable/ANSI-ish baseline: double-quoted `"identifier"` quoting with -- a genuine, material difference from every other `TYPE` variant's own quoting, all of which escape an embedded quote by doubling it -- *no* escape mechanism for an embedded quote at all (directly confirmed: `CREATE TABLE "weird""name"` fails outright with ORA-25716, "The identifier contains a double quotation mark"), so an embedded quote shall be stripped rather than doubled. `NUMBER(19, 4)` for VFP currency (an exact match for its own fixed 4-decimal-digit scale), `INTEGER` (Oracle's own documented `NUMBER(38)` ANSI-compatible subtype), `BINARY_DOUBLE` for VFP's own 8-byte double (Oracle's true IEEE 754 type, an exact width match unlike arbitrary-precision `NUMBER`), `NUMBER(1)` for logical with `1`/`0` literals (Oracle has no dedicated table-column `BOOLEAN` type before Oracle 23c; directly confirmed that even there, a `NUMBER(1)` column's own `TRUE` literal silently converts to `1` rather than requiring the new syntax, so this exporter targets the one literal form every Oracle version accepts identically), `VARCHAR2(length CHAR)` -- not a bare byte-counted `VARCHAR2(length)` -- for character fields, since Oracle's own default `NLS_LENGTH_SEMANTICS=BYTE` would otherwise reject a real multi-byte UTF-8 value (this codebase decodes legacy DBF text to UTF-8 for every dialect) that fits within `length` characters (directly confirmed against the real engine: a 10-byte `VARCHAR2(10)` rejects 10 non-ASCII characters decoding to 20 UTF-8 bytes with ORA-12899, while explicit `VARCHAR2(10 CHAR)` accepts them regardless of session settings); and `CLOB` -- not `VARCHAR2`'s 4000-byte-default-capped text -- for memo/general/picture and any other unrecognized storage type, with the CLOB column's own literal built as `EMPTY_CLOB()` for a blank value (directly confirmed a plain `''` literal silently becomes `NULL` for a real CLOB column, corrupting a genuinely blank memo) or one-or-more concatenated `TO_CLOB('...')` chunks each within Oracle's own confirmed 4000-*byte* (not character) SQL text-literal limit, split on a UTF-8 character boundary via the same `utf8_safe_truncate()` byte-mode this exporter's own identifier truncation already uses so a chunk boundary can never split a multi-byte character. A non-null empty `C`/`V` value shall fail the whole export closed with a localized `Vfp.AssetInspector.Validation.OracleEmptyCharacterValueUnrepresentable` diagnostic naming the table and column, rather than report a successful export whose ordinary load silently changes that value's own non-null-ness (#5693, found by an automated Codex code-review pass after #5564's own original slice closed): directly confirmed against a real local Oracle 23ai engine that a zero-length `VARCHAR2` literal (`INSERT INTO ... VALUES ('')`) is stored as `NULL`, not an actual empty string (`IS NULL` reports `TRUE`) -- unlike a blank Date (which already has a real `NULL` fallback with no distinction lost) or a blank Memo (which `CLOB`'s own `EMPTY_CLOB()` already represents distinctly from `NULL`), no `VARCHAR2` literal at all exists that preserves a genuinely non-null empty Character/Varchar value's own non-null-ness. A table with no `_NullFlags` field has no nullable fields at all, so this narrowing does not affect the check's own applicability to #5693's own demonstrated repro. A VFP date/datetime value shall be wrapped in Oracle's own ANSI-style `DATE 'YYYY-MM-DD'`/`TIMESTAMP 'YYYY-MM-DD HH:MM:SS'` literal syntax rather than emitted as a plain quoted string the way every other `TYPE` variant's own D/T handling does -- directly confirmed that a bare ISO-shaped string is *not* safe here (`INSERT ... VALUES ('2026-01-15')` into a `DATE` column fails with ORA-01861, "literal does not match format string", against the real engine's own default `NLS_DATE_FORMAT` of `DD-MON-RR`, not `YYYY-MM-DD`), while the ANSI literal form is documented to always parse in exactly that ISO shape regardless of session settings. A blank VFP date shall resolve to `NULL` rather than a quoted empty string -- unlike `RQ-CF-MODERNIZATION-009`'s own SQL Server, where the empty-string form merely silently invents `1900-01-01`, Oracle's own `DATE ''` is invalid syntax outright (directly confirmed: ORA-01841, "(full) year must be between -4713 and +9999, and not be 0"), so this is a basic correctness requirement here, not merely a data-integrity improvement. No per-tag index uniqueness constraint, ascending/descending sort order, or `FOR` (filtered/partial) index translation is captured or claimed. Only a same-base-name production `.cdx` is consulted -- single-file `.idx`/`.ndx`/`.mdx`/`.ntx` companions are not scanned. MySQL's own `utf8mb4` character-set/collation defaults are relied upon implicitly (the exporter emits no explicit `CHARACTER SET`/`COLLATE` clause on `CREATE TABLE`, unlike Oracle's own explicit `CHAR`-length-semantics fix) -- a target server configured with a legacy non-`utf8mb4` default character set could still reject or mangle a genuinely non-ASCII VFP value even though this exporter's own generated DDL is otherwise correct, a real, documented gap rather than an oversight, mirroring `RQ-CF-MODERNIZATION-009`'s own analogous non-`NVARCHAR` limitation. `AUTO_INCREMENT`, foreign-key constraints, and storage-engine selection (`InnoDB` vs. `MyISAM`) are not emitted or claimed; the target server's own default storage engine is relied upon implicitly. This exporter's own quoting/type/literal choices were verified only against MySQL 8.0 specifically; a differently-configured server (e.g. `NO_BACKSLASH_ESCAPES` or `ANSI_QUOTES` explicitly enabled, or a pre-8.0 MySQL/MariaDB fork with different `DECIMAL`/identifier-limit behavior) was not independently re-verified, a real, documented gap rather than an oversight -- this exporter, like every sibling `TYPE <VENDOR>` variant in this family, targets each engine's own out-of-the-box default configuration. The #5582 PR review's overlong-table/column-name finding is a genuinely shared gap across this whole family, not MySQL-specific: `RQ-CF-MODERNIZATION-007`/`RQ-CF-MODERNIZATION-008`/`RQ-CF-MODERNIZATION-009`/`RQ-CF-MODERNIZATION-010` (PostgreSQL/SQLite/SQL Server/Oracle) each already have their own real, hard identifier-length limit (63 bytes, no limit claimed for SQLite, 128 characters, 128 bytes respectively) but validate it only for their own *generated* index names, never for a table's own pre-existing name from the DBC catalog -- this row's own fix does not extend to those four sibling dialects; a follow-up issue should be filed to track applying the identical fail-closed check there. #5697 (found by an automated Codex code-review pass): a zero-field cataloged member table now fails this exporter closed too, sharing `write_mysql_tables_and_data()`'s existing `hard_failure_error` out-parameter (renamed from `identifier_too_long_error` since it now serves two distinct hard-failure reasons) -- see `RQ-CF-MODERNIZATION-003`'s row for the full description. #5698 (found by an automated Codex code-review pass): a non-blank numeric value that cannot be safely represented now fails this exporter closed too, sharing `write_mysql_tables_and_data()`'s existing `hard_failure_error` out-parameter -- see `RQ-CF-MODERNIZATION-003`'s row for the full description. | `defined` |

It shall preserve localized human `header.version_description` output separately. The classification identifies only a file-format family and shall not claim execution compatibility, write support, full asset preservation, historical-runtime emulation, or binary production. | `include/copperfin/vfp/dbf_header.h`; `src/vfp/dbf_header.cpp`; `apps/copperfin_inspect/main.cpp` | `tests/test_vfp_assets.cpp`; `tests/test_dbf_header_robustness.cpp`; `tests/test_localization_host_inspect.cpp`; broader protected native validation remains release evidence | Focused asset tests prove representative VFP, dBASE including Level 7 and a memo-flag value, FoxBase, and unknown classifications; the bounded robustness suite invokes the accessor across every version-byte value; the inspect-host locale test proves the machine field stays `visual_foxpro` while human descriptions localize. | `HZ-data-corruption-01`; read-only identification only. | `RQ-CF-LEGACY-002` | Repository-owner #5482 dBASE-family read-only migration slice; dBASE's public [Version 7 file structure](https://www.dbase.com/Knowledgebase/INT/db7_file_fmt.htm), including its 68-byte fixed header, 48-byte descriptors, packed records, DBT pointers, and type encodings; dBASE III/IV layout details cross-checked against the MIT-licensed `infused/dbf` fixture artifacts recorded in `tests/fixtures/legacy-dbase-infused/README.md` | The table reader shall recognize the `dbase` header family and select its documented physical layout rather than applying VFP descriptor semantics: dBASE III/IV fields are physically packed after the deletion marker and shall not use the descriptor's in-memory address as a file offset; dBASE 7 shall parse its 68-byte preamble and 48-byte descriptors and reflect its independent memo/SQL bits in header diagnostics. It shall read dBASE III's text-form DBT block pointers and `0x1A 0x1A`-terminated payloads, and dBASE IV/7's text-form pointers with little-endian length-prefixed DBT payloads. It shall interpret dBASE `B` as a DBT pointer (not VFP's double), dBASE `I`/`+` as big-endian sign/magnitude Long/Autoincrement values, dBASE `O` as an IEEE double, and dBASE `@` as the documented Julian-day/millisecond timestamp representation. A missing dBASE descriptor terminator or a missing dBASE III memo terminator shall fail closed without consuming arbitrary structure or memo bytes. Every mutating public DBF operation shall reject the `dbase` family before any write. | `src/vfp/dbf_header.cpp`; `src/vfp/dbf_table.cpp`; `include/copperfin/vfp/dbf_table.h`; `tests/fixtures/legacy-dbase-infused/` | `tests/test_dbf_table.cpp` (`test_parse_real_dbase_family_fixtures`, `test_dbase_legacy_layouts_fail_closed_when_truncated`, `test_dbase_tables_reject_mutation_without_touching_source_bytes`); `tests/test_vfp_assets.cpp` | Focused tests cover unmodified dBASE III (`0x03`), dBASE III + DBT (`0x83`), dBASE IV + DBT (`0x8b`), and Level-7 memo-flag (`0x8c`) artifacts, including packed-offset field recovery, both DBT framing schemes, and dBASE-7 Autoincrement decoding. They also prove the Level-7 memo/SQL bit combinations report the correct sidecar/diagnostic state, a missing Level-7 descriptor terminator rejects parsing, an unterminated dBASE-III memo produces only an unresolved-block placeholder, and a rejected mutation preserves every source byte. Full protected platform validation remains required. | `HZ-data-corruption-01`; read-only table/field inspection only. No dBASE table, DBT, or index mutation; no Clipper-family handling (`RQ-CF-LEGACY-003` covers the FoxBASE/FoxPro slice); no historical runtime execution; and no claim of full DBF7 field-property, index, or DBT binary/OLE interpretation. dBASE binary `B`/`G`/`P` payloads are exposed only as deterministic bytes, not interpreted. | `defined` |
| `RQ-CF-LEGACY-003` | Repository-owner #5483 FoxBASE/FoxPro read-only migration slice; FoxBASE/FoxPro's documented DBF dialect deltas from both dBASE and VFP9 ([dBASE II header/descriptor layout](https://www.fileformat.info/format/dbf/corion-dbase-ii.htm); [DBF version-signature table](https://www.dbf2002.com/dbf-file-format.html)), cross-checked against the MIT-licensed `infused/dbf` fixture artifacts recorded in `tests/fixtures/legacy-foxpro-infused/README.md` | The table reader shall recognize the `foxbase` (`0x02`, `0xfb`) and `foxpro` (`0xf5`) header families and select their documented physical layouts. FoxBASE's `0x02` variant has no 32-byte dBASE III header at all: its main header is 8 bytes (a 2-byte record count, then one byte each for last-update month/day/year -- not dBASE III's year/month/day order -- then a 2-byte record length; no header length, table-flags, or code-page byte), the descriptor table is always a fixed 521-byte allocation regardless of declared field count, and each 16-byte descriptor has an 11-byte name, 1-byte type, 1-byte length, a 2-byte in-memory field-address slot meaningless on disk, and a real 1-byte decimal-count -- fields are packed sequentially with no on-disk offset. `0xfb` is also a documented FoxBASE signature and shall classify and describe identically to `0x02`. Its true physical layout was never confirmed by any primary source (see #5528's research, `docs/70-foxbase-0xfb-investigation.md`), so rather than guess it shares `0x02`'s distinct 8-byte structure, the reader shall use the same sequential-packing layout already verified for `0x03` (dBASE III) -- the lineage every informed source that ventured a guess at `0xfb`'s meaning converged on -- instead of the raw VFP-native default layout it would otherwise incidentally fall through to. FoxPro (`0xf5`) descriptors already store the correct on-disk physical field offset the same way VFP does, so the reader shall keep VFP's offset and FPT block-header/block-size handling for FoxPro, but its `M`/`G`/`P` memo and General pointers are dBASE-style right-justified ASCII decimal text, not VFP's 4-byte little-endian binary pointer. A missing FoxBASE (`0x02`) descriptor terminator, or a FoxBASE/FoxPro field offset/length that exceeds the declared record length, shall fail closed without reading past the record. The memo-block-size probe used by runtime cursor snapshots shall not scan `foxbase`-family descriptors with the VFP-only 32-byte layout. Every mutating public DBF operation shall reject the `foxbase` and `foxpro` families before any write. | `src/vfp/dbf_header.cpp`; `src/vfp/dbf_table.cpp`; `tests/fixtures/legacy-foxpro-infused/` | `tests/test_dbf_table.cpp` (`test_parse_real_foxbase_foxpro_family_fixtures`, `test_foxbase_header_date_bytes_and_0xfb_classification`, `test_foxbase_foxpro_layouts_fail_closed_when_truncated`, `test_foxbase_memo_block_size_probe_does_not_misread_descriptors`, `test_foxbase_foxpro_tables_reject_mutation_without_touching_source_bytes`, `test_dbf_reader_uses_dbase_iii_layout_for_0xfb`); `tests/test_vfp_assets.cpp` | Focused tests cover an unmodified 9-record FoxBASE artifact (`0x02`, no memo) and a 975-record FoxPro artifact with an FPT sidecar (`0xf5`), including FoxBASE's sequential physical offsets and its real per-field decimal-count byte (verified against the bundled fixture's `PAYRATE`/`START:PAY` fields, both declared with 3 decimals), a synthetic header proving the month/day/year last-update byte order, `0xfb` classifying and describing identically to `0x02` while never reporting a memo file, a real dBASE III fixture version-byte-patched to `0xfb` decoding identically to the same bytes read as `0x03` (proving the #5528 layout routing), and FoxPro's ASCII-text memo/General pointer decoded against VFP's length-prefixed FPT block layout. They also prove a missing FoxBASE descriptor terminator and an out-of-range FoxPro field offset both reject parsing, that the memo-block-size probe returns nothing for a FoxBASE table instead of misreading its descriptors, and that a rejected mutation preserves every source byte on both families. Full protected platform validation remains required. | `HZ-data-corruption-01`; read-only table/field inspection only. No FoxBASE/FoxPro table, memo, or index mutation; no FoxBASE/FoxPro index reading; no Clipper-family handling (`RQ-CF-LEGACY-002`/`-003` cover dBASE and FoxBASE/FoxPro only); no historical runtime execution. FoxPro `G`/`P` General/Picture payloads are exposed only as deterministic bytes, not interpreted. `0xfb`'s physical header/descriptor layout was never confirmed by any primary source -- #5528's research (`docs/70-foxbase-0xfb-investigation.md`) traced the widely-repeated "0xFB = FoxBASE" claim back to a community reference table that marks it an acknowledged unknown ("FoxPro ???"), found no real specimen ever documented in ~20 years of active xBase cataloging, and confirmed a fabricated AI-assistant citation claiming otherwise. Since every informed guess converges on the same lineage as the already-verified `0x03` byte, repository owner decided (issue #5528) to read `0xfb` using that same sequential-packing layout (`dbf_read_layout()`) rather than the raw VFP-native default it previously fell through to -- a documented best-effort choice, not a verified fact, subject to revision if a real specimen ever surfaces. The version byte alone does not distinguish FoxBASE from FoxBASE+, nor FoxPro 1.x from later FoxPro 2.x releases -- this slice verifies the `foxbase`/`foxpro` header families generically rather than a specific named product generation; no FoxBASE+-only or FoxPro-1.x-only artifact or behavior was separately obtained or verified. | `defined` |

| `RQ-CF-LEGACY-006` | Repository-owner #5527, prompted by real FoxBASE+ 2.10 (not an emulator or reconstruction) evidence: a table last updated 2026-09-09 has DBF header byte 1 = `0x1A` (26 decimal), confirming real dBASE-family products write the on-disk last-update-year byte as a genuine two-digit calendar year (`year % 100`), not the "years since 1900" framing the DBF format's own nominal documentation implies (a theoretical 1900-2155 range); web research corroborated this against multiple independent DBF-format references | `DbfHeader::last_update_iso8601()` shall disambiguate an on-disk last-update-year byte below a fixed threshold of 80 as `2000 + byte`, and a byte at or above 80 (covering both genuine two-digit years from dBASE-family software's active era of roughly 1980-1999, and any byte >= 100 produced by Copperfin's own pre-#5527 writer) as `1900 + byte`. The threshold shall be fixed, not evaluated relative to the current wall-clock date the way Visual FoxPro's own `SET CENTURY TO ... ROLLOVER` default (current two-digit year + 50) is for live date-string parsing -- that "now"-relative convention is the wrong fit for this specific field, since genuine legacy dBASE-era files were only ever written during a historically fixed window that does not advance with the calendar, and a sliding threshold would eventually misclassify them once enough years pass. `stamp_dbf_last_update_date()` (the writer) shall stamp the genuine two-digit calendar year (`year % 100`) rather than the raw C `tm_year` value (years-since-1900, unbounded up to 255) it wrote before this fix, so that files Copperfin itself writes for any post-1999 date are byte-compatible with what real dBASE-family software would write for the same date, rather than only self-consistent with Copperfin's own prior reader. Review (Codex, Copilot -- duplicate P1 finding) found that `LUPDATE()` (`src/runtime/prg_engine_records.inl`) had its own independent, unfixed `1900 + last_update_year` computation, so it silently disagreed with `last_update_iso8601()` about the same file's year for any post-1999 two-digit byte; the disambiguation logic was extracted into a new shared `DbfHeader::last_update_year_full()` accessor (`include/copperfin/vfp/dbf_header.h`/`src/vfp/dbf_header.cpp`) and `LUPDATE()` now routes through it, so every consumer of `last_update_year` agrees. | `src/vfp/dbf_header.cpp` (`DbfHeader::last_update_iso8601`, `DbfHeader::last_update_year_full`); `include/copperfin/vfp/dbf_header.h`; `src/vfp/dbf_table.cpp` (`stamp_dbf_last_update_date`); `src/runtime/prg_engine_records.inl` (`LUPDATE()`) | `tests/test_vfp_assets.cpp` (`test_last_update_iso8601_applies_fixed_century_rollover_for_real_foxbase_byte`); `tests/test_dbf_table.cpp` (`test_stamp_dbf_last_update_date_writes_real_dbase_two_digit_year_convention`); `tests/test_prg_engine.cpp` (`test_lupdate_reports_full_calendar_year_matching_last_update_iso8601`) | Focused tests prove the real FoxBASE+ 2.10 byte (26) now resolves to 2026 rather than the pre-fix 1926; a PRG-level regression test proves `LUPDATE()` now agrees with `last_update_iso8601()` for the same file rather than silently disagreeing past 1999; that a byte >= 100 (Copperfin's own pre-#5527 writer convention) still resolves unambiguously as `1900 + byte`, unchanged from before this fix, so files it already wrote remain correctly readable; and that a byte at or above the fixed 80 threshold (a real 1991 FoxBASE fixture's byte, 91) resolves to the 1900s and will keep doing so indefinitely, rather than eventually flipping to the 2000s the way a "now"-relative rollover would. A writer round-trip test proves `stamp_dbf_last_update_date()` now writes a byte < 100 for the real current date, checked against the actual wall-clock date at test-run time (a legitimate self-consistency check, not a hardcoded assertion that decays). Full protected platform validation remains required. | `HZ-data-corruption-01`; this is an inherent-ambiguity heuristic, not a verified-correct mapping for every possible byte -- any two-digit-year scheme cannot distinguish, for example, a genuine 1905 file from a genuine 2005 file if both existed, though no such conflict is realistic here since dBASE-family software did not exist before ~1980. The fixed 80 threshold remains correct for the realistic byte range (1980-1999 legacy, 2000-2079 modern/Copperfin) but will eventually need revisiting for dates from 2080 onward, the same long-horizon limitation any fixed two-digit-year scheme has. No sliding/dynamic rollover was chosen; see this row's own requirement text for why. | `defined` |

| `RQ-CF-AGENT-005` | Explicit repository-owner product policy; derived from `RQ-CF-AGENT-001` and its audit/revocation hazards | Before any mutable executor is connected, native session authority shall exist only after the current activation policy allows the request and a content-free start audit event is committed with a nonempty receipt. The versioned event JSON shall remain machine-readable and byte-stable under process-global locale changes. One active session shall bind one generation, effective mode, exact admitted capabilities, and its receipt; another start shall not replace or expand it. Policy-evaluation failure shall grant no authority, produce an audited denial when the sink remains available, and restore the transition for later activation. Stop shall clear authority before invoking its audit sink, and audit failure shall remain visible without extending authority. An exception raised anywhere after stop begins its transition and before that transition is cleared (e.g. an allocation failure or an OS mutex/condition-variable primitive throwing) shall restore the transition to idle before propagating, exactly as an equivalent exception during start already does, so a single fault cannot permanently deny every later start/cleanup/lease-acquisition call. An inactive snapshot shall retain no capability or receipt. The lifecycle contract shall not authenticate providers, execute tools, access workspaces, or expose a command-line activation path. | `docs/64-workspace-agent-access-policy.md`; `include/copperfin/security/workspace_agent_session.h`; `include/copperfin/security/workspace_agent_session_test_hooks.h`; `src/security/workspace_agent_session.cpp` | `tests/test_workspace_agent_session.cpp`; policy, localization, and native-isolation regressions; protected Windows, Ubuntu, and macOS execution required | `docs/safety/traceability-report-2026-08-14-workspace-agent-session.md`; exact implementation head `65bb48915`; protected runs `31827637259`, `31827638217`, `31827638262`, `31827638271`, and `31827638315`; local Release and sanitizer evidence recorded. 2026-08-31 addendum (issue #5401): `stop()`'s post-transition-flag section lacked `start()`'s equivalent catch-and-reset guard; fixed with a matching `catch (...) { transition_ = idle; throw; }` wrap. Verified locally via a dedicated test-only fault-injection hook (the real trigger -- allocation/OS-primitive failure -- is not reproducible deterministically through the public API); the added regression was confirmed to fail without the fix by temporarily reverting it and observing all three of its assertions fail, then confirmed to pass once restored. `test_workspace_agent_session` and the broader security battery (`test_security_controls`, `test_workspace_agent_target_containment`, `test_workspace_agent_process_containment`, `test_workspace_agent_isolated_environment`, `test_workspace_agent_audit_sink`, `test_bounded_wide_string`) pass 7/7 locally on Linux. A first adversarial `/code-review` pass on that fix found it incomplete: the `WorkspaceAgentSessionSnapshot` copy (`revoked_session = active_session_;`, string-owning, genuinely throw-capable) ran immediately after `transition_ = stopping` but was still outside that `try` block, reproducing the same bug one statement earlier. Fixed by replacing all three hand-copied `catch(...)` guards (`start()`, `cleanup_pending_session_layout()`, `stop()`) with one shared RAII `ResetOnExit<T>` helper armed immediately after each method sets its non-idle transition value, before any other work in that critical section -- closing the finding structurally, addressing the review's related point that the hand-copied pattern was itself the kind of omission that produced the bug. Re-verified the regression catches this by temporarily disabling the guard's `arm()` call and observing the same three assertions fail, then restoring it. 7/7 passes again locally; full Windows Native Validation dispatch (run `33354458540`) passed against the exact pre-round-3 head. A second adversarial `/code-review` pass on the `ResetOnExit` fix found two further real issues: (1) `ResetOnExit` had no `disarm()`, so every one of `start()`/`cleanup_pending_session_layout()`/`stop()`'s own success-path explicit `transition_ = Transition::idle;` was followed, after the enclosing lock released, by the still-armed guard's destructor re-acquiring the mutex and unconditionally re-writing the same field on ordinary function exit -- an unnecessary unlock/re-lock window in which a concurrent thread's legitimate transition (started after the explicit reset, before the guard's redundant one) could be silently clobbered back to idle, undermining `RQ-CF-AGENT-005`'s "another start shall not replace or expand it" invariant; (2) the guard's destructor performed `std::lock_guard lock(mutex_)` with no exception handling, and since destructors are implicitly `noexcept`, `std::mutex::lock()` throwing (a real if rare possibility per its own contract) would call `std::terminate()` and crash the whole process -- a strictly worse failure mode than the pre-refactor hand-written `catch(...)` blocks, which ran in ordinary function scope where the same failure would have propagated as a catchable exception instead. Fixed by adding `ResetOnExit::disarm()`, called immediately after each of the three methods' own explicit final reset (inside the same lock as that write), and by wrapping the destructor's lock/reset in `try`/`catch(...)` that swallows rather than lets an exception escape -- on that path `target_` is deliberately left at its non-idle value, which is safe because every session entry point already fails closed (denies further start/stop/cleanup) while it is non-idle, so an unrecoverable mutex failure degrades to permanently fail-closed rather than terminating the process. `test_workspace_agent_session` and the broader security battery pass 7/7 again locally; fresh Windows Native Validation dispatch (run `33359322307`) passed against that head. A third adversarial `/code-review` pass found the test-only fault-injection hook (`workspace_agent_session_test_hooks.h`) had no macro gate, unlike every other test-only hook in this codebase (`COPPERFIN_ENABLE_RUNTIME_PIPELINE_TEST_HOOKS`, `COPPERFIN_RUNTIME_HOST_TEST_HOOKS`): `cf_security` is linked into every production executable (`copperfin_runtime_host`, `copperfin_studio_host`, `copperfin_mcp_host`, `copperfin_inspect`), so the setter shipped in production, letting any code with call access into the process force the next `stop()` call anywhere to throw at the exact point `transition_` had just changed but authority had not yet been revoked. Fixed by gating the hook variable, its check-and-fire call site, the setter's definition, and the header's whole declaration behind a new `COPPERFIN_ENABLE_WORKSPACE_AGENT_SESSION_TEST_HOOKS` macro, defined only on `cf_security` (and the `test_workspace_agent_session` test target) when `COPPERFIN_BUILD_TESTS` is on -- matching the existing `cf_runtime_pipeline`/`copperfin_runtime_host` convention. Verified the fix actually closes the gap, not just moves it: configured and built a separate `-DCOPPERFIN_BUILD_TESTS=OFF` tree and confirmed via `nm` that the hook symbol is entirely absent from the resulting `libcf_security.a`. The review's second point -- the hook is one process-global atomic shared by every controller instance, safe only because tests run sequentially on one thread -- was already documented as a known constraint in the header's doc comment and is deferred rather than restructured into a per-instance seam, which would be a materially larger change to a security-critical class's private state for a currently-hypothetical (tests are not parallelized) risk. `test_workspace_agent_session` and the broader security battery pass again locally; fresh Windows Native Validation dispatch is running before merge. | `HZ-system-failure-01`; `HZ-data-corruption-01`; no executor, real sandbox, provider/OAuth adapter, persistent audit sink, trusted consent UI, product stop control, or session indicator is claimed | `defined` |

An allow shall expose only canonical path and point-in-time identity and shall not read file content, create, mutate, delete, reserve, or authorize the target. | `RQ-CF-CONTAINMENT-001` | Explicit repository-owner product security policy; derived from `RQ-CF-AGENT-009`, `RQ-CF-AGENT-010`, `RQ-CF-AGENT-012`, and data-corruption/system-failure hazards found via code review of the shared containment primitive after those requirements' own alias-rejection fix (owner review, 2026-08-30) | The shared physical-path-containment primitive used by every workspace-agent boundary and by package-content installation shall verify that no path component beneath a trusted root is a symlink, reparse point, or cross-device mount, and shall bind the returned canonical path and physical identity to the exact filesystem object that verification observed, with no window in which a component could be replaced between being verified and being used. On POSIX this shall be enforced by opening each path component in sequence via `openat()` with `O_NOFOLLOW`, carrying the previous component's descriptor forward as the directory the next component is opened relative to, so component verification and component use are the same operation; final identity and the returned canonical path shall be derived from that verified descriptor, not from a separate, later path-string resolution. On Windows, which has no descriptor-relative-open primitive without native NT APIs, this shall instead avoid a second, wholesale re-resolution of the same untrusted relative path after the reparse-point walk, so a component swapped after that walk starts is caught by the next component's own check on current filesystem state rather than surviving into an independent later pass; this narrows rather than eliminates the window and Windows attacker models requiring full atomicity remain a gap. | `include/copperfin/security/physical_path_containment.h`; `src/security/physical_path_containment.cpp` | `tests/test_security_controls.cpp` (symlink/reparse-point file and directory targets, package-root aliasing, stale/replaced-path identity checks); `tests/test_workspace_agent_target_containment.cpp`; `tests/test_workspace_agent_process_containment.cpp`; `tests/test_workspace_agent_isolated_environment.cpp`; `tests/test_workspace_agent_audit_sink.cpp`; `tests/test_runtime_pipeline.cpp` (package content-io containment); protected Windows, Ubuntu, and macOS execution required | Local Linux (GCC/Clang) focused evidence: all six directly and indirectly dependent test binaries build warning-free and pass; two real defects found and fixed during initial development (an `ENOTDIR`/`ELOOP` errno-classification gap surfaced only by actually exercising the existing directory-symlink regressions, and a use-after-close descriptor bug caught by re-review before any test run). Two subsequent adversarial `/code-review` passes against the pushed branch found and fixed 6 further real issues before merge: a FIFO-triggered DoS from a blocking final-component `openat()` (both in the check walk and in the separate read function, `O_NONBLOCK` added to both, the second now covered by a dedicated `mkfifo`-based regression test in `test_security_controls.cpp` bounded with a timed `std::async` so a reintroduced hang fails the test rather than the suite); an over-strict Windows root reparse-point rejection that would have broken legitimate package-alias-as-root usage; a Windows post-walk safety-net check that was tautological because it re-derived the canonical path by string concatenation instead of reading it back from the verified handle (fixed via `GetFinalPathNameByHandleW`, which in turn needed its extended-length `\\?\` namespace prefix stripped to match `canonical_root`'s spelling -- itself caught by the second review pass after the first pass's fix introduced it); and an `ENOTDIR` failure-reason misclassification (diagnostic-only). One further finding -- `read_physically_contained_file_snapshot()` reopening the verified path by string rather than reusing the check phase's descriptor/handle -- was confirmed to predate this change (same architecture as the prior baseline) and is deferred as issue #5409 rather than expanding this fix's scope; the identity comparison that function already performs (device+inode/volume-serial+file-index, not merely size/mtime) bounds the residual window to an inode-reuse-with-matching-identity attack, not a plain component swap. Protected Windows/macOS CI evidence pending before merge. 2026-08-31 addendum (issue #5409, slice #5420): added `inspect_and_open_physically_contained_path()` and `read_physically_contained_file_snapshot_from_handle()`, an additive-only opt-in pair that closes the residual window structurally rather than incidentally -- the check-phase `openat()`/`CreateFileW` walk's own verified descriptor/handle is now carried forward (via a new move-only, PIMPL'd `PhysicalPathContainmentHandle` that keeps this public header free of platform-selection tokens, per the lesson from PR #5418's `scoped_resource.h` fix) and read from directly, so the object read can never differ from the object the check verified, for any reason, on any platform (Windows and every POSIX target -- Linux, macOS, and other POSIX systems -- share the same code path here; only Windows needed a signature change, widening the walk's final-component access from `FILE_READ_ATTRIBUTES` to `FILE_GENERIC_READ` only when a caller opts in, since POSIX's existing `O_RDONLY` already permits both). No existing call site changed in this slice (see #5421/#5422 for planned migrations); `inspect_physical_path_containment()` itself was refactored to share the same underlying walk (`inspect_and_walk()`) so there is one source of truth, not two. A real defect surfaced only by writing the new regression test, not by design review: the before/after identity re-check inside the new handle-based read initially reused the full `PhysicalPathIdentity::operator==` (unchanged from the string-reopening function), which includes `link_count` -- since the regression test's swap scenario legitimately unlinks the checked path (dropping the still-open descriptor's `st_nlink` from 1 to 0 without touching a single byte of content, verified via a standalone repro), this made the handle-based read spuriously fail exactly the substitution scenario it exists to survive. Fixed with a dedicated `content_equal()` comparison method on `PhysicalPathIdentity` (storage/file id, size, mtime; deliberately excluding `link_count`) used only by the new handle-based path -- the string-reopening function's `link_count` check remains unchanged and correct for its own, different threat model (detecting a different object being resolved by the reopen). Verified the fix and the regression test's own validity by temporarily reverting the comparison back to the full identity check and confirming the new test fails, then restoring it. An adversarial `/code-review` pass found three further real issues before merge: (1) the handle-based read never reset the handle/descriptor's file position, so a second call on the same handle (nothing prevents this -- it takes the handle by `const&`) would read from wherever the first call left off and spuriously fail with `identity_changed` on the resulting truncated read; fixed by seeking to the start unconditionally at the top of every read, with a new regression test proving a second call reads the whole object again; (2) on Windows, a `GetFileInformationByHandle()` API failure and an actual reparse-point detection were both reported as `indirect_component`, which would misreport a transient API failure as a symlink-attack rejection in security audit logs once callers migrate to this function; split into `read_failed` for the API failure and `indirect_component` only for a genuine reparse point; (3) the link-count-excluding comparison duplicated `PhysicalPathIdentity::operator==`'s field list in a different file, risking silent drift; fixed by moving the shared comparison onto `PhysicalPathIdentity` itself as a new `content_equal()` method, with `operator==` now defined in terms of it. A second adversarial review pass, using a compiled concurrent-access harness under ASan/UBSan, empirically reproduced a real race the first review round's own fix introduced: the shared file-position cursor reset at the top of every call (round-2's fix for repeat-call correctness) meant concurrent calls from separate threads on the same handle raced on that cursor, corrupting 1 to 5 of 8 concurrent reads across repeated runs. Fixed by switching to position-independent reads (`pread()` on POSIX; offset-based `ReadFile()` via `OVERLAPPED`, explicitly handling `ERROR_HANDLE_EOF`, on Windows) so each call tracks its own offset locally with no shared mutable cursor state -- this also obsoletes the round-2 seek fix. Added an 8-thread concurrent-read regression test; verified it reliably catches the race (10/10 failures) by temporarily reintroducing the shared-cursor pattern, then confirmed clean (10/10 passes) after restoring the fix. The same pass found the new handle-based read duplicates most of the read-loop/identity-recheck structure already in the pre-existing string-reopening read function, and that the "query native info -> reject reparse/directory -> build identity" sequence is now written three times in this file; deliberately deferred as issue #5424 rather than expanding this fix's scope into the pre-existing, already twice-reviewed string-reopening function's internals. A third adversarial review pass found the handle-based read's returned containment was the stale, check-time identity snapshot rather than the freshly re-verified post-read one the function already computes and discards; several existing callers use the returned `link_count` as a security gate against hardlink-based confinement bypass, and since `content_equal()` deliberately excludes `link_count` from the freshness check, a hard link added during the read window would have been silently invisible to a caller relying on the returned snapshot. Fixed by returning the already-computed post-read identity instead, with a new regression test proving the returned `link_count` reflects a hard link added after the check but before the read. Also unified the `InternalContainmentWalk`/`PhysicalPathContainmentHandle::Impl` duplicate struct pair via inheritance, and fixed a stale reference to the renamed `content_identity_matches()` helper (now `content_equal()`) earlier in this row. 2026-08-31 addendum (issue #5421): migrated `workspace_agent_process_parser.cpp`'s two check-then-read pairs (`capture_binding()`, `authorize_windows()`) to the handle-based primitive. Adversarial review found a real regression the migration introduced: both functions previously got an effective post-read `link_count` re-check "for free" via the string-reopening function's full `operator==` comparison, but the handle-based read's `content_equal()` freshness check deliberately excludes `link_count` (by design, per this row's #5420 addendum) -- so a hard link added to the trusted executable during the read window would have gone undetected, silently weakening this file's link-count-based anti-confinement-bypass invariant. Fixed with a shared `read_trusted_executable_snapshot()` helper used by both functions that re-checks the fresh post-read identity's `link_count != 1` before either trusts `snapshot.ok`; added a macro-gated test hook (`COPPERFIN_ENABLE_WORKSPACE_AGENT_PROCESS_PARSER_TEST_HOOKS`, mirroring the PR #5416 precedent) and a dedicated regression test injecting a hard link mid-read, later fixed to skip gracefully on hard-link-unsupported filesystems rather than hard-failing the suite. 2026-09-01 addendum (issue #5422, closed as not-applicable): verified before migrating rather than assuming the #5421 pattern transferred -- `workspace_agent_target_containment.cpp`'s only inspect-then-read in the same function checks two *different* objects (the workspace root, then a target file whose identity was captured by a separate, earlier call from a different top-level API entry point across an async, audit-logged tool-call round trip), not a tight pair; migrating would mean holding a live OS handle open across that round trip, which would be worse than the existing string-reopening design's already-thorough before/after full-identity comparison plus independent post-read re-walk. Re-surveyed every `read_physically_contained_file_snapshot()` call site in the codebase (not just this row's originally-named candidates) and found two genuine tight pairs the original triage missed, tracked as #5426 (`runtime_pipeline_launcher_artifact_inventory.cpp`) and #5427 (`polyglot_supporting_artifact_admission.cpp`'s `admit_polyglot_supporting_artifact()`; that file's `revalidate_polyglot_supporting_artifact_admission()` has the same admission-to-execution-gap shape as the closed #5422 site and is intentionally left unmigrated). 2026-09-01 addendum (issue #5426): migrated `runtime_pipeline_launcher_artifact_inventory.cpp`'s `admit_launcher_artifact()` check-then-read pair. This call site has no `link_count`-dependent trust invariant (the computed SHA256 digest is stored only in the build-time launcher-artifact inventory, never compared against a captured identity's `link_count` downstream), so unlike #5421 no additional `link_count` post-read re-check was needed beyond the handle-based read's own `content_equal()` freshness check -- confirmed by reading every use of the digest before migrating, not assumed. An adversarial review pass on the PR before merge found a different real gap: `read_physically_contained_file_snapshot_from_handle()` guarantees the bytes read match the object the walk verified, but unlike the string-reopening function it replaces, makes no claim the *path* still resolves to that object once the read completes -- another process renaming/replacing the artifact mid-read would let the handle-bound read still succeed (correctly reading the original object), while the resulting inventory entry (keyed by file name) would record a digest for an object the package no longer ships under that name. Fixed by restoring an independent post-read `inspect_physical_path_containment()` re-walk at this call site, mirroring what the string-reopening function did. This is a property of this call site's shape (a name-keyed inventory with no later re-verification step), not a defect in the shared primitive itself -- #5421's callers already re-verify independently, by design, immediately before any eventual use. A second adversarial review pass on the same PR found the fix's re-walk used the raw pre-walk path (`*exact`) rather than the already-verified `containment.canonical_path`, so despite claiming to mirror `read_physically_contained_file_snapshot()`'s own post-read check it was actually re-doing directory-entry resolution from scratch; fixed to re-walk `containment.canonical_path`. Also found the fix had no dedicated regression test (added `test_launcher_artifact_admission_rejects_rename_during_read()` with a new macro-gated `set_launcher_artifact_post_read_test_hook()`, verified to catch the regression) and is hand-inlined rather than factored into a shared helper -- deferred as issue #5434 since #5427 will need the identical pattern. A third adversarial review pass found the round-2 fix used the stricter full-identity comparison (including link_count) rather than content_equal(), contradicting this call site's own comment that it has no link_count-dependent invariant, and risking a spurious build failure on a benign transient link_count change; fixed to use content_equal(), re-verified the regression test still passes and still catches the rename/replace case. A second finding (this function reuses one generic error code across six failure modes with no retry for transient races) was confirmed pre-existing, not a regression, and deferred as issue #5435. | `HZ-data-corruption-01`; `HZ-system-failure-01`; issue #5400; POSIX close to fully atomic via `O_NOFOLLOW` component chaining for the check-only path (`inspect_physical_path_containment()`), fully atomic end-to-end for the new opt-in check-and-read path (`inspect_and_open_physically_contained_path()` + `read_physically_contained_file_snapshot_from_handle()`) on both platform families; Windows check-only path narrows but does not eliminate the window absent native NT API use; a sufficiently privileged local attacker able to swap filesystem objects at kernel/driver level remains out of scope for any userspace mitigation; call-site migration complete for #5421 and #5426; #5422 closed as not-applicable (async admission/read gap, not a tight pair); #5427 (`polyglot_supporting_artifact_admission.cpp`) done. 2026-09-01 addendum (issue #5427): migrated `admit_polyglot_supporting_artifact()`'s check-then-read pair. Unlike #5426, no post-read path re-walk was needed -- the digest is compared inline against an independently caller-supplied `expected_sha256`, so a substituted object is already caught by the hash-mismatch check; confirmed by reasoning through the threat model before migrating, not assumed. No `link_count`-dependent invariant exists in this file (grepped for it). `revalidate_polyglot_supporting_artifact_admission()`'s read is intentionally left unmigrated, same admission-to-execution-gap shape as the closed #5422 site. An adversarial review pass on the PR before merge found this call site needed the same post-read path re-walk fix as #5426 after all: the inline expected_sha256 comparison protects the bytes read via the handle but not resolved_path_'s continued binding to that object once the function returns, since a future/other caller is not guaranteed to revalidate before use the way the one current caller does. Fixed identically to #5426 (content_equal() re-walk), with a new macro-gated test hook and regression test, verified to catch the regression. A second adversarial review pass found the re-walk's denial reused containment_denied, conflating a genuine TOCTOU-attack signal with the mundane initial-denial meaning that code has elsewhere in the function; fixed to use the existing artifact_changed value (already used by this file's own revalidate function for analogous checks) with a new changed_during_admission diagnostic, mirroring the sibling polyglot_artifact_admission.cpp's established convention; tightened the regression test to assert the specific error code. | `defined` |

| `RQ-CF-AGENT-031` | Explicit repository-owner product security policy; derived from `RQ-CF-AGENT-026`, `RQ-CF-AGENT-028`, `HZ-system-failure-01`, and `HZ-data-corruption-01` | This derived requirement supersedes only `RQ-CF-AGENT-026`'s POSIX unlink-before-success detail, for macOS specifically. macOS supports neither relinking an already-unlinked file back into the namespace (confirmed empirically: `linkat()` sourcing a hardlink from a `/dev/fd` path onto a zero-link inode fails with `EPERM`) nor a true `fexecve()` analog (its `/dev/fd` workaround for exec-by-descriptor only permits path lookups from the same process that opened the descriptor directly, denying a forked child that merely inherited it via `fork()` -- confirmed empirically: `fstat()` on the inherited descriptor succeeds and reports the correct file, while `access()`/`execve()` via its `/dev/fd` path both fail identically with `EACCES`). On macOS, after the exact bytes have been written and reread against the original approved span, the platform boundary shall instead relax the newly created file to owner read/write, ad-hoc code-sign it in place by invoking `/usr/bin/codesign` directly by fixed path with no shell and no PATH search, and restore owner read/execute-only mode. It shall retain the resulting linked, code-signed descriptor and real path for the image's entire lifetime rather than unlinking, and shall destroy that linked file only when the retained image object itself is destroyed. Exec, when it occurs, shall use that real path directly rather than any `/dev/fd` indirection. A same-UID actor that tampers with the file after signing shall be refused execution by the kernel's own code-signature enforcement (AMFI) at the moment of exec, not by any filesystem permission, flag, or ACL this boundary itself could apply and that same actor could equally relax by virtue of owning the file. Signing failure, reopen failure, or any exact-byte mismatch found before signing shall return no image and retain no partial authority, identically to `RQ-CF-AGENT-026`. Same-object, exact-byte-before-signing, opacity, destruction-order, and invariant-denial requirements remain; POSIX behavior on non-macOS hosts, actual controller execution, working-directory entry, sandbox, endpoints/descendants, outcome audit, and promotion remain unchanged and unavailable. | `docs/64-workspace-agent-access-policy.md`; `include/copperfin/platform/private_executable_image.h`; `src/platform/private_executable_image.cpp`; `src/platform/bounded_process.cpp`; `src/platform/bounded_process_private.h`; `tests/run_workspace_agent_process_image_materialization_contract_check.cmake` | `tests/test_workspace_agent_isolated_environment.cpp`; `tests/test_platform_private_directory.cpp`; exact-snapshot materialization contract; protected macOS generated-launcher execution required | Local focused Debug CTest evidence on Linux: full contract/session/parser/isolated-environment/private-directory set passes except the pre-existing, separately-tracked `RQ-CF-AGENT-028` callback-side-fork gap (issue #5493) -- this change is macOS-only and Linux behavior is provably unchanged (identical unlink-then-`fexecve()` path, same literal `status.st_nlink == 0` check retained).
